To integrate data across models and libraries, we used Chronos23, which corrects for copy number (CN) effects, screen quality and library-specific batch effects (Fig. 1h,i). Because all NextGen models used the Humagne library, library-specific batch effects were confounded with organoid model effects. We therefore pre-trained Chronos on all traditional and NextGen CNS models before applying the pretrained parameters to organoids. The integrated dataset—encompassing WGS, RNA-seq and CRISPR data and growth-format annotations—is available through the DepMap portal (https://DepMap.org/portal)24.
A similar WNT pathway dependency is known in traditional PDAC models with RNF43 LOF49. However, in organoids, these dependencies correlated with the PDAC-classical/MUC transcriptional state irrespective of RNF43, APC, CTNNB1 or AXIN2 mutations (Extended Data Figs. 7k and 8a,b). All 19 organoids strongly dependent on the five listed WNT regulators (average Chronos dependency score < –1) were PDAC-classical/MUC-high (expression score > 2.8), including those with and without WNT-activating genetic alterations (Extended Data Fig. 8b). This result suggests that enhanced WNT dependency is driven by the transcriptional cell state rather than genetic alterations.
To broadly compare gene essentialities, we calculated the difference in mean dependency scores between 3D and 2D models for every gene (n = 16,691; excluding common essential genes), and identified gene sets (Hallmark and KEGG gene sets from MSigDB; q < 0.05) that were significantly overrepresented at the extremes of this difference (Fig. 5a,b). Clustering these gene sets by component overlap (>0.3; Extended Data Fig. 8h) merged two sets (adhesion and cytoskeleton (adhesion–cytoskeleton), and cell cycle), whereas five others (lipid metabolism, mTORC1, oxidative phosphorylation, UV response and systemic lupus erythematosus) remained separate.Fig. 5Comparing 2D and 3D dependencies to disentangle the effects of culture conditions.a,b, Gene sets enriched for differential dependencies between 3D and 2D models (n = 94 (3D) and 433 (2D)). Odds ratios for the 9 significant gene sets (q < 0.05) overrepresented in the 200 outlier genes were plotted, clustered and coloured by gene overlap (>0.3; a). Mean dependency differences and their significance for individual genes (two-tailed Mann–Whitney U-test; gene n = 3,521), coloured by the gene set clusters from a were also plotted (b). c–e, Minipool screens were performed to elucidate the differing effects of growth format and culture medium. A minipool screen (250 test genes) was designed (c) to separate the effects of growth format (plastic versus dome) and culture medium (RPMI–FBS versus OPAC) on gene essentiality. The screens identified genes for which dependencies are primarily altered by growth format (d) or culture medium (e), with red dashed lines indicating significance threshold (q = 0.05, two-sided linear regression t-test) and the point colours indicating gene classes in the minipool library (bottom). Screen n = 11 (dome), 7 (plastic), 9 (OPAC), 9 (RPMI–FBS). f,g, Comparisons across growth formats and culture media. f, Different gene classes represented in the minipool library are highlighted. Integrin and actin regulation classes exhibited significant variance across growth formats. g, By contrast, lipid metabolism class were affected by culture medium (q < 0.05, two-tailed F-test; gene n = 200). h, Differential effects on integrin and cholesterol homeostasis dependencies assessed by viability after test gene knockout. Values are the mean ± s.d. (n = 3). P values calculated using two-tailed two-way ANOVA. After applying Chronos to the minipool screen data (which correlated well with genome-wide data, r > 0.59; Extended Data Fig. 9c), we used linear regression to distinguish growth-format and culture-medium effects. This analysis identified seven genes for which dependencies were altered by growth formats (q < 0.05), including three integrin subunits (ITGB1, ITGAV and ITGA3) and two actin regulators (NCKAP1 and PTK2; Fig. 5d and Extended Data Fig. 9d). Eight genes also exhibited dependency changes across different media, three of which regulate lipid metabolism (SPTLC1, SPTLC2 and SQLE; Fig. 5e and Extended Data Fig. 9e).
All screens were initially evaluated for sequencing quality and selected to have a mean of at least 185 reads per gene. Naive gene effect scores were calculated by taking the log of the ratio of normalized reads for each gene in screened samples to that of normalized reads for each gene in the corresponding plasmid library. For screens using the two Humagne C and D libraries, the log fold change scores of libraries were averaged together. An ROC-AUC was calculated on the basis of per cent of true positive hits (essential genes with log fold change below –1) for every possible false discovery rate. An NNMD was calculated for each screen by taking the difference between the average log fold changes for essential and non-essential genes and dividing by the standard deviation of the non-essential genes69. Screens that had an NNMD greater than –1.25 or a correlation between replicates less than 0.19 were excluded from downstream analyses. Chronos scores were then calculated using the open-source code Chronos (v.2.0.8; https://github.com/broadinstitute/chronos) as previously described23 for each type of guide library (Avana spCas9, KY spCas9 or Humagne CD enAsCas12a) independently. To mitigate batch effects between guide libraries, the mean Chronos score per gene in individual libraries was regularized towards the global mean across all libraries. On the DepMap portal, the file ‘ScreenGeneEffect’ contains Chronos gene effect estimates for each individual screen, with library effects corrected out whereas organoid-specific effects are preserved. Beginning with the 26Q1 release, a similar strategy will be used to preserve organoid-specific effects in ‘CRISPRGeneEffect’, which integrates all screens of the same model into a model-indexed table that can be used with portal tools.
To identify dependencies with expression addiction, we scored the Pearson’s correlation coefficient (r) and the significance (P) of correlation between the expression level (log2[TPM + 1]) and dependency (Chronos gene effect) of the same gene across all the NextGen models that were profiled by both RNA-seq and CRISPR screening (n = 146) (Fig. 2a). This process was conducted for all the genes that have expression data and were classified as strong dependency (strongly selective, high variance or pan-dependency) in the NextGen model CRISPR screen dataset (n = 3,786). The false discovery rate (or q value) was calculated using the Benjamin–Hochberg procedure for this and subsequent analyses unless otherwise indicated. The genes that met the criteria for significance (r < −0.3 and q < 0.005) were identified as dependencies with expression addiction. For each of the strong dependency genes identified from the NextGen model data, the correlation between expression and dependencies was also computed across the profiling results of the traditional cell lines that have lineage-matched NextGen models and gene expression data (n = 572).
Paralogue dependencies were identified by calculating the Pearson’s correlation coefficient and the significance of correlation between the dependency (Chronos gene effect) on a gene and the expression level (log2[TPM + 1]) of its paralogue partner (Fig. 2d). We conducted this process for all the strong dependency genes in the NextGen model CRISPR screen dataset (n = 3,800), comparing their dependency with the expression level of the respective paralogue partners listed on Ensembl genome browser 113 (https://www.ensembl.org/). Paralogue pairs were extracted via BioMart query for all human paralogues with valid HGNC symbols. Only gene pairs with at least 25% sequence identity between paralogues and with variance in expression >1 over all the NextGen models were considered for analyses. The dependency gene–paralogue pairs that met the significance criteria (r < –0.3 and q < 0.005) were identified as paralogue dependencies. Here again, we computed correlations for the same dependency–expression paralogue pairs in the traditional model profiling results (n = 572).
Minipool screens were evaluated for sequencing quality by having a total read count of at least 500,000 reads across replicates. Gene scores and NNMDs were computed in the same manner as the genome-wide screens. Replicate correlation was computed as the Pearson’s correlation coefficient over log fold changes in all non-control genes. Screens with NNMDs greater than –1.5 and replicate correlations less than 0.2 were excluded from downstream analyses. Screens with a single replicate were retained if their other metrics passed these quality control thresholds. Chronos scores were calculated as previously described23, with adjusted hyperparameters (smoothing regularization parameter = 0.3, kernel width = 5) for the smaller library size. Subsequently, we modelled the Chronos score Yg,k for each gene g and screen k with the generalized linear regression model:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal}

For Fig. 5a, the top 100 genes in either extreme of the distribution of mean dependency differences (3D organoids (n = 94) versus lineage-matched 2D models (n = 433)) were combined to form a set of 200 genes with differential dependencies. Gene sets from the Hallmark and KEGG legacy collections were tested for enrichment among this set of differential dependency genes using hypergeometric test (or one-sided Fisher’s exact test). The gene sets that achieved significance at q < 0.05 were considered as significantly overrepresented in differential dependency genes. These gene sets were tested for the mutual overlap of their constituent genes. Gene sets exhibiting high-degrees of overlap (overlap coefficient > 0.3) were merged with each other (Extended Data Fig. 8h).
For Fig. 5d,e, in the minipool screen analysis, the effects of growth format (dome – plastic) and culture medium (OPAC – RPMI–FBS) on gene dependency (Chronos score) were determined as coefficients in a linear regression model (see Methods, ‘Minipool screen analysis’), which was also used for calculating the P values against the null hypothesis that these variables have no effect on gene dependency (two-sided linear regression t-test). The number of screens for each condition is as follows: dome format, n = 11; plastic format, n = 7; OPAC medium, n = 9; RPMI–FBS medium, n = 9.
For Extended Data Fig. 8h, overlap coefficients were calculated between individual groups of the 9 gene sets with significant overrepresentation of the 200 outlier genes in the distribution of mean dependency differences (Fig. 5a). The numbers of member genes in each of these gene sets are as follows: 200 (Hallmark oxidative phosphorylation); 200 (Hallmark MTORC1 signalling); 200 (Hallmark G2M checkpoint); 139 (KEGG systemic lupus erythematosus); 158 (Hallmark fatty acid metabolism); 200 (Hallmark E2F targets); 199 (KEGG focal adhesion); 213 (KEGG regulation of actin cytoskeleton); and 158 (Hallmark UV response up). Gene sets exhibiting high degrees of overlap (overlap coefficient > 0.3) were merged with each other.
For Extended Data Fig. 9d,e, for the seven pairs of minipool screens that were conducted with the same model, same medium but different growth format (dome versus plastic), the Chronos dependency scores of all the test genes in the minipool library (n = 250) were compared using two-tailed paired t-tests (d). Similarly, for the five pairs of minipool screens that were conducted with the same model, same format but different culture medium (OPAC versus RPMI–FBS), the Chronos dependency scores of all the test genes in the minipool library were also compared using two-tailed paired t-tests (e). All genes with significant differential dependencies in either of these comparisons as well as CDK4 and GPX4 are shown in the plots.

Extended Data Fig. 4Comparison of gene expression and CRISPR screen outcomes between 2D and 3D cultures.a,b, CRISPR screen QC metrics. In a, ROC-AUC (Receiver Operating Characteristic Area Under the Curve) scores for common essential (positive controls; defined as the intersection of essential genes in Hart et al.70 and Blomen et al.71) and nonessential (negative controls; defined by Hart et al.70) controls were calculated using the naïve gene effect scores and plotted for all the DepMap CRISPR screens. In b, NNMD (Null-Normalized Median Difference) score between the common essential and nonessential controls was calculated for each screen. The screens evaluated here included Cas9 traditional model screens (n = 1,351), enAsCas12a traditional model screens (n = 36), enAsCas12a NextGen organoid screens (n = 108), and enCas12a NextGen CNS model screens (n = 39). For both metrics, scores from the NextGen models were comparable to those from the Cas9 traditional model screens and enAsCas12a traditional model screens. The centre (for consistency with the main text) line and box denote median and 25th to 75th percentile data points, respectively, and whiskers extend up to 1.5 times the interquartile range from box edges. c,d, Clustering of samples from NextGen CNS tumor models in 3D spheroid culture and 2D adherent culture on Matrigel-coated plates (“coat” culture) by the correlation of gene expression. Nine NextGen CNS tumor spheroid models were either maintained in the spheroid culture or propagated in the coat culture for > 4 weeks. In c, gene expression profiles of these 18 samples (9 models x 2 conditions) were compared and clustered using the correlation of expression across top 10% highly variable genes (n = 1,919). These samples were primarily clustered by cell identity, but not by the culture conditions. The distributions of correlation between two different samples were also plotted (d), exhibiting the high degree of correlation (r > 0.8) between two samples with different conditions (spheroid and adherent cultures) of the same model. P-values were calculated with two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test. n = 9 (same model under coat and spheroid culture conditions), 72 (different models under different culture conditions), 36 (different models both in coat cultures), and 36 (different models both in spheroid cultures). e,f, High concordance of gene expression between spheroid and coat cultures of the NextGen CNS models. The mean expression levels in the nine spheroid culture samples (x-axis) and the nine coat culture samples (y-axis) were plotted for all the genes (n = 19,193), which exhibited a very high degree of concordance (r = 0.996) between these two culture conditions (e). The difference in the mean expression levels (x-axis) and the statistical significance of the difference (by a two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test; y-axis) were also plotted (f). The false discovery rate (FDR; q) was 1 for all the genes, indicating that none of the genes exhibited statistically significant differences. g,h Concordance of Chronos dependency scores between 3D culture and 2D coat culture. The mean dependency scores of each gene in the NextGen CNS models screened under 3D spheroid cultures (n = 14; x-axis) and in the NextGen CNS models screened under adherent cultures on laminin- or Matrigel-coated plates (“coat” cultures; n = 25; y-axis) were plotted (g, left). Similarly, the mean dependency scores of genes in the organoid models screened under 3D Matrigel dome cultures (n = 55; x-axis) and in the organoid models screened under coat cultures (n = 14; y-axis) were plotted (g, right). For the organoids in Matrigel dome cultures, only the models from lineages that have corresponding coat-cultured models (ampulla of vator, colorectal, oeso-stm, ovary, prostate and uterus) were included in this comparison. In both cases, a high degree of concordance was observed between the two different conditions of culture (r = 0.990 and 0.984 for CNS models and organoids, respectively). The difference in the mean dependency scores between the spheroid and coat culture of the CNS models (h, left) and between the dome and coat cultures of the organoid models (h, right) (x-axis) and the significance of the difference (y-axis; by a two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test) were also plotted. In both cases, none of the genes exhibited a statistically significant difference of the mean dependency scores between two different culture conditions (q = 1 for all the genes [CNS]; q > 0.71 for all the genes [organoids]). Note that gene expression comparison between spheroid and coat cultures of the NextGen CNS models (c-f) were conducted with matched model groups, while the comparison of CRISPR outcomes (g,h) were performed between unmatched, different groups of models.

Extended Data Fig. 5Dependencies linked to gene expression identified in NextGen vs traditional models.a, Representative distribution of dependency scores for each dependency category. Each gene evaluated by the CRISPR screen was classified into either of these five dependency classes based on the distribution of its Chronos dependency scores across DepMap NextGen models (or organoid models). We used a previously published strategy for this classification25 (also see Methods). b, Correlation of expression and dependency for PROX1 and ERBB2 in the NextGen (left) and traditional (right) models. Elevated expression of PROX1 correlated with enhanced dependency on its own more strongly in the NextGen models (r = −0.462) than in the traditional cell lines (r = −0.182). In contrast, while the traditional cell lines exhibit very strong association (r = −0.530) between the expression and dependency levels of ERBB2, this relationship was much weaker (r = −0.077) in the NextGen models. Here and in e, regression lines with 95% confidence intervals are displayed. c, Increased expression of HNRNPH1 and PROX1 and reduced expression of ENO2 in tumors relative to lineage-matched normal tissues. The expression levels of HNRNPH1, PROX1 and ENO2 in the tumor tissues and corresponding normal tissues were analyzed per lineage in the combined TCGA/TARGET/GTEx dataset downloaded from the Xena browser (https://xenabrowser.net/). n = 9 (biliary tract, normal), 36 (biliary tract, tumor), 1,151 (CNS-brain, normal), 689 (CNS-brain, tumor), 355 (colorectal, normal), 383 (colorectal, tumor), 152 (prostate, normal), and 496 (prostate, tumor). Expression values were plotted for the lineages with tumor-specific upregulation (HNRNPH1 and PROX1) or downregulation (ENO2) of these genes. The centre line and box denote median and 25th to 75th percentile data points, respectively, and whiskers extend up to 1.5 times the interquartile range from box edges. P-values were scored by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test. d, Reduced expression of ESR1/ERBB2 underlies the absence of expression addiction for these oncogenes in the NextGen breast cancer models. The association between the expression and dependency of two breast cancer oncogenes, ESR1 and ERBB2, was plotted for the NextGen (green circles) and traditional (navy circles) breast cancer models. None of the 14 breast cancer organoids exhibited high (log2[TPM + 1] > 3.4) expression of ESR1, while 11 out of the 51 traditional breast cancer models showed high expression of this oncogene. All the traditional cell lines with strong (Chronos score < −1) ESR1 dependency displayed high expression of ESR1. Similarly, only one out of the 14 NextGen models, and 14 out of the 51 traditional cell lines, exhibited high expression of ERBB2 (log2[TPM + 1] > 7.5) and the majority (10/12) of the traditional cell lines whose screen results exhibit strong ERBB2 dependencies were found to have high ERBB2 expression. Hence, the absence of expression addiction relationship for ESR1 and ERBB2 in the NextGen breast cancer models appears to be attributable, in part, to the reduced expression of these oncogenes in these models as compared to the levels observed in their traditional counterparts. e, ELMO2 paralog dependency in the NextGen (left) vs traditional (right) models. ELMO2 dependency was correlated strongly (r = 0.545) with reduced expression of its paralog ELMO3 in the traditional cell lines, while this relationship was not observed (r = −0.110) in the NextGen models.

Extended Data Fig. 6Enhanced CDK6 dependency linked to glial gene expression of NextGen GBM models.a, Differential clustering of NextGen and traditional GBM models on the Celligner embedding. Celligner-based comparison of overall gene expression patterns (see Fig. 3b) showed that NextGen GBM models and traditional GBM models are clustered separately. The cluster with the majority of NextGen models was named as “glial”, while the other cluster enriched with most of the traditional cell lines was labeled as “mesenchymal”. The number of models in each of these clusters per model type (NextGen or traditional) and growth pattern (adherent, mixed or spheroid) was plotted. b. Expression levels of SOX2 and OLIG2, two markers for the glial subtype GBM, between NextGen and traditional GBM models. The NextGen models exhibited significantly higher expression of both markers than the traditional cell lines (P < 4 ×10−16 by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test). c, Differential activation of gene expression meta-programs (MPs) between GBM models in the glial vs mesenchymal clusters. A gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) was applied to the differential gene expression data between the GBM models of the glial and mesenchymal clusters to compare expression levels of genes defining each of the 41 MPs (see Fig. 3b,d). The five neuronal lineage-specific MPs, including the “Astrocyte” program (left), were selectively activated by models in the glial cluster, while other programs, including the “EMT-II” program (right), were expressed specifically by models in the mesenchymal cluster. d, Expression levels of the five neuronal lineage-specific MPs in DepMap GBM models and TCGA-plus GBM tumors. For all these five neuronal MPs, GBM tumors (n = 180) and glial GBM models (n = 67) exhibited comparable levels of expression, which were far higher than the expression levels observed in the mesenchymal GBM models (n = 54). The centre line and box denote median and 25th to 75th percentile data points, respectively, and whiskers extend up to 1.5 times the interquartile range from box edges. Here and in e, f, P-values were scored by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test. e, Differential dependency analysis comparing glial GBM models (n = 36) and all other non-glial organoids and spheroids from 10 lineages (n = 115). Selective dependencies were defined as genes with q < 0.05 (horizontal dashed line) and gene effect < −0.3 (vertical dashed line) and highlighted in maroon. f, CDK6 dependency association with mutation. For the mutations (hotspot or damaging) with ≥ 5 GBM screens of mutant models (five different mutations), CDK6 dependency was compared between mutant and nonmutant models, and the difference in mean Chronos dependency scores (x-axis) and the significance of difference (y-axis) were plotted (screen n = 95). Significant dependency difference was not observed for any of these five mutation types (q > 0.32). g, Correlation between CDK6 dependency and gene copy numbers (CNs) across DepMap glial GBM models. Correlation of CDK6 dependency with log2-transformed CN values (log2[relative to ploidy +1]) of the genes within the top 10% high variance of CN across DepMap glial GBM models (with CRISPR scores; screen n = 36) was plotted. Genes on Chr7q and Chr9p21.3 are indicated by maroon and blue dots, respectively. P-values were scored by two-tailed Pearson correlation. h,i, Viability of CDKN2A CN loss glial GBM models after CDK6/CDK4 knockout. Two NextGen glial GBM models with CDKN2A CN loss—BT145 (CDKN2A log2[relative to ploidy+1] = 2.54 ×10−6) and BT1718 (CDKN2A log2[relative to ploidy+1] = 1.91 ×10−6), both of which were also engineered to express Cas12a, were infected with lentivirus expressing the indicated sgRNAs and successfully infected cells were reseeded at the indicated densities into a 24-well plate. These cells were subjected to viability assay 10 days after the infection. The viability relative to the cells receiving a cutting control sgRNA (sgCh2) are plotted (h). These scores were also normalized to the average viability of cells transduced with the cutting control sgRNA (set as 0) and the average viability of cells with knockout (KO) of a pan-dependency gene (sgSF3B1; set as −1), and the normalized values for the samples with the low seeding density (50k cells/well) were plotted (i). Values = means ± SD (n = 4). P-values were scored by two-tailed Student’s t-test. j,k, Inhibition of cell growth by CDK4/6 inhibitor treatment of the NextGen GBM models. Seven NextGen glioblastoma models, among which two models (BT187 and BT224; blue lines) were CDKN2A copy number (CN) neutral while the remaining five models (red lines) had loss of CDKN2A CN, were treated with various concentrations of CDK4/6 inhibitors (Abemaciclib, Palbociclib, and Ribociclib). In j, the relative viabilities of the drug-treated cells against the mock control (growth rate [GR] value) were plotted. Distributions of the drug-treated cells to different phases of cell cycle were also plotted for one CDKN2A CN neutral model (BT224) and one CDKN2A CN loss model (BT286) (k).
Source data


a, Differential clustering of NextGen and traditional GBM models on the Celligner embedding. Celligner-based comparison of overall gene expression patterns (see Fig. 3b) showed that NextGen GBM models and traditional GBM models are clustered separately. The cluster with the majority of NextGen models was named as “glial”, while the other cluster enriched with most of the traditional cell lines was labeled as “mesenchymal”. The number of models in each of these clusters per model type (NextGen or traditional) and growth pattern (adherent, mixed or spheroid) was plotted. b. Expression levels of SOX2 and OLIG2, two markers for the glial subtype GBM, between NextGen and traditional GBM models. The NextGen models exhibited significantly higher expression of both markers than the traditional cell lines (P < 4 ×10−16 by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test). c, Differential activation of gene expression meta-programs (MPs) between GBM models in the glial vs mesenchymal clusters. A gene set enrichment analysis (GSEA) was applied to the differential gene expression data between the GBM models of the glial and mesenchymal clusters to compare expression levels of genes defining each of the 41 MPs (see Fig. 3b,d). The five neuronal lineage-specific MPs, including the “Astrocyte” program (left), were selectively activated by models in the glial cluster, while other programs, including the “EMT-II” program (right), were expressed specifically by models in the mesenchymal cluster. d, Expression levels of the five neuronal lineage-specific MPs in DepMap GBM models and TCGA-plus GBM tumors. For all these five neuronal MPs, GBM tumors (n = 180) and glial GBM models (n = 67) exhibited comparable levels of expression, which were far higher than the expression levels observed in the mesenchymal GBM models (n = 54). The centre line and box denote median and 25th to 75th percentile data points, respectively, and whiskers extend up to 1.5 times the interquartile range from box edges. Here and in e, f, P-values were scored by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test. e, Differential dependency analysis comparing glial GBM models (n = 36) and all other non-glial organoids and spheroids from 10 lineages (n = 115). Selective dependencies were defined as genes with q < 0.05 (horizontal dashed line) and gene effect < −0.3 (vertical dashed line) and highlighted in maroon. f, CDK6 dependency association with mutation. For the mutations (hotspot or damaging) with ≥ 5 GBM screens of mutant models (five different mutations), CDK6 dependency was compared between mutant and nonmutant models, and the difference in mean Chronos dependency scores (x-axis) and the significance of difference (y-axis) were plotted (screen n = 95). Significant dependency difference was not observed for any of these five mutation types (q > 0.32). g, Correlation between CDK6 dependency and gene copy numbers (CNs) across DepMap glial GBM models. Correlation of CDK6 dependency with log2-transformed CN values (log2[relative to ploidy +1]) of the genes within the top 10% high variance of CN across DepMap glial GBM models (with CRISPR scores; screen n = 36) was plotted. Genes on Chr7q and Chr9p21.3 are indicated by maroon and blue dots, respectively. P-values were scored by two-tailed Pearson correlation. h,i, Viability of CDKN2A CN loss glial GBM models after CDK6/CDK4 knockout. Two NextGen glial GBM models with CDKN2A CN loss—BT145 (CDKN2A log2[relative to ploidy+1] = 2.54 ×10−6) and BT1718 (CDKN2A log2[relative to ploidy+1] = 1.91 ×10−6), both of which were also engineered to express Cas12a, were infected with lentivirus expressing the indicated sgRNAs and successfully infected cells were reseeded at the indicated densities into a 24-well plate. These cells were subjected to viability assay 10 days after the infection. The viability relative to the cells receiving a cutting control sgRNA (sgCh2) are plotted (h). These scores were also normalized to the average viability of cells transduced with the cutting control sgRNA (set as 0) and the average viability of cells with knockout (KO) of a pan-dependency gene (sgSF3B1; set as −1), and the normalized values for the samples with the low seeding density (50k cells/well) were plotted (i). Values = means ± SD (n = 4). P-values were scored by two-tailed Student’s t-test. j,k, Inhibition of cell growth by CDK4/6 inhibitor treatment of the NextGen GBM models. Seven NextGen glioblastoma models, among which two models (BT187 and BT224; blue lines) were CDKN2A copy number (CN) neutral while the remaining five models (red lines) had loss of CDKN2A CN, were treated with various concentrations of CDK4/6 inhibitors (Abemaciclib, Palbociclib, and Ribociclib). In j, the relative viabilities of the drug-treated cells against the mock control (growth rate [GR] value) were plotted. Distributions of the drug-treated cells to different phases of cell cycle were also plotted for one CDKN2A CN neutral model (BT224) and one CDKN2A CN loss model (BT286) (k).

Extended Data Fig. 8Extended analyses of Wnt dependency and other cancer gene dependencies.a, Lack of constant association between Wnt-activating genetic alteration and Wnt regulator-dependency. All the CRISPR-screened organoids (n = 108) categorized into Wnt pathway gene alteration-positive (n = 30) or -negative (n = 78) groups based on mutations/CN alterations in CTNNB1, APC, AXIN2, or RNF43. The positive group included 21 APC, 1 AXIN2, 5 CTNNB1 hotspot, 5 RNF43 damaging mutations, and 1 CTNNB1 CN amplification, with some models harboring multiple alterations. Dependencies on the Wnt signaling pathway regulators (MESD, WLS, TCF7L2, FZD5 and LGR4) were plotted for each of these two groups (left). The expression levels of the PDAC-classical/MUC program were also plotted. For the Wnt pathway gene alteration-positive (red box) group, the point colors indicate the type of alteration. Significance of the difference was assessed by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test. The centre line and box denote median and 25th to 75th percentile data points, respectively, and whiskers extend up to 1.5 times the interquartile range from box edges. Harboring Wnt-activating alterations did not have constant effects on Wnt regulator dependencies: it reduced the dependencies on MESD, WLS, FZD5 and LGR4, but enhanced TCF7L2 dependency. b, Mutation-independent association between PDAC-classical/MUC program expression and dependencies on Wnt regulators. The correlation between PDAC-classical/MUC program expression and dependency on the five regulators of Wnt signaling (MESD, WLS, TCF7L2, FZD5 and LGR4) as well as their mean dependency scores in the organoid models were plotted, where Wnt-activating genetic alterations were indicated by the color code shown in a. All the organoids with strong mean dependency scores on these Wnt regulators (mean Chronos score < −1; n = 21) exhibited enhanced PDAC-classical/MUC program expression (expression score > 2.8) regardless of Wnt-activating genetic alterations. Regression lines with 95% confidence intervals are also displayed. c, Wnt dependency in the traditional cell lines and organoids. The mean dependency in the organoids (n = 108; y-axis) and lineage-matched traditional models (screen n = 485; x-axis) were plotted for all the members of the MSigDB ‘GOBP_WNT_SIGNALING_PATHWAY’ gene set. For the seven members that exhibited significant correlation with the PDAC-classical MP expression (MESD, WLS, TCF7L2, FZD5, LGR4, MARK2 and SOX9; see Fig. 4d), the NextGen organoids exhibited stronger dependency than lineage-matched traditional cell lines (mean[NextGen] > mean[traditional]). However, this held true for less than a half of the remaining members of this gene set (198/438 [45.2%]). d,e, Differential dependencies on frequently mutated oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes (TSGs) between 2D models and 3D organoids. Dependency on frequently mutated (>1% mutation rate in the TCGA cohort53) oncogenes and TSGs (40 oncogenes and 50 TSGs) were compared between the 3D organoids grown in serum-free conditions (n = 94) and lineage-matched 2D adherent models cultured directly on plastic plates with serum-containing media (n = 433). In d, the mean dependency scores in 2D vs 3D models are represented in the x- and y-axes, respectively. Here and in f, the color of the points indicates the type of the cancer genes (red: oncogenes, blue: TSGs) and the point sizes indicate the significance of the difference. Only 22 oncogenes and 20 TSGs that exhibited statistically significant (q < 0.05) difference of dependency were shown here, while in c, the pathways displaying significant and non-significant (q ≥ 0.05) differences are distinguished by filled and open circles, respectively. We also compared the dependency on each of these cancer genes after removing the mutant (hotspot mutation for oncogenes; damaging mutations for TSGs) models for the respective gene, but it remained significant for all the 42 genes shown here. In e, the distribution of dependency in individual models were plotted for two representative cancer genes (CDK4 and MDM2), where the lineage was indicated by the point color (see g for the legend). For MDM2, the dependency distribution in TP53 WT models (n = 22 [3D organoids], 97 [2D models]) were also plotted (right). Here and in g, the statistical significance was assessed by two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test. f,g, A pathway-level summary of the differential dependencies on frequently mutated oncogenes and TSGs. Oncogenes and TSGs analyzed in a were classified into the 10 oncogenic signaling pathways in accordance with a previous publication53. For each of these 10 pathways, mean dependency scores on the oncogenes or TSGs that belong to the respective pathway were calculated and these mean scores were compared between 3D (y-axis; n = 94) and 2D (x-axis; n = 433) models (f). The pathway:cancer gene type (oncogene or TSG) pairs with two or more genes were plotted. The mean dependency scores of the individual models were also plotted for the TSGs of the p53 pathway (g). h, Clustering of the gene sets that are enriched for differential dependencies between 2D models and 3D organoids. In the global comparison of gene dependencies, the nine gene sets with significant overrepresentation (q < 0.05 by one-sided Fisher’s exact test) of the 200 outlier genes (top 100 genes in either extreme) in the distribution of mean dependency differences (see Fig. 5a) were clustered by the degree of overlap in their component genes. Note that this analysis was conducted not only on the selected cancer genes but on all the genes measured in the screen except for the common essential genes (as opposed to d-g). The number of member genes within each of these gene sets is: 200 (HALLMARK_OXIDATIVE_PHOSPHORYLATION), 200 (HALLMARK_MTORC1_SIGNALING), 200 (HALLMARK_G2M_CHECKPOINT), 139 (KEGG_SYSTEMIC_LUPUS_ERYTHEMATOSUS), 158 (HALLMARK_FATTY_ACID_METABOLISM), 200 (HALLMARK_E2F_TARGETS), 199 (KEGG_FOCAL_ADHESION), 213 (KEGG_REGULATION_OF_ACTIN_CYTOSKELETON), and 158 (HALLMARK_UV_RESPONSE_UP). Two pairs of gene sets exhibited a high-degree of overlap (overlap coefficient > 0.3) with each other and therefore we merged each of them into one gene set: • KEGG_FOCAL_ADHESION and KEGG_REGULATION_OF_ACTIN_CYTOSKELETON (merged and renamed as ‘Adhesion/Cytoskeleton’) • HALLMARK_G2M_CHECKPOINT and HALLMARK_E2F_TARGETS (‘Cell Cycle’) The remaining five gene sets, HALLMARK_FATTY_ACID_METABOLISM (‘Lipid Metabolism’), HALL

Extended Data Fig. 9CRISPR minipool screens in different growth formats and medium types.a, Composition of test genes in the minipool library (n = 250). The library for the minipool screen was designed primarily to evaluate the essentiality of genes that exhibit large differences in the systematic comparison of gene dependencies between 2D models and 3D organoids (see Fig. 5a,b). These genes include those involved in the regulation of adhesion/cytoskeleton (further divided into “ECM Adhesion”, “Actin Regulation”, “Tight Junction”, “Adherens Junction” and “Integrin” classes) and the control of cholesterol homeostasis (as a part of the “Lipid Metabolism” class). They were categorized into nine core classes that are listed on the left based on the function of the encoded protein. Some of the test genes belong to multiple classes, while others do not belong to any of these classes but are still included in the library based on specific interests. The membership patterns of individual genes across the nine functional classes, along with the count of test genes exhibiting each specific pattern, are shown (top). The number of test genes that belong to each of the nine classes were also plotted (left). b, Quality control (QC) test of individual minipool screens. Seven models were subjected to screening with the minipool library under different combinations of growth formats (adherent culture on plastic dishes [plastic] and Matrigel dome culture [dome]) and culture media (RPMI/FBS and OPAC serum-free media). 18 screens passed the criteria for QC, which were total readcounts ≥ 5 ×106 and null-normalized mean difference (NNMD) ≤ −1.5. These QC metrics for individual screens as well as the log fold change (LFC) correlation of replicate samples (where replicate samples were available) are presented. c, Correlation between the genome-wide screen results and minipool screen results. The dependency scores from the genome-wide CRISPR screen (x-axis) and minipool screen (y-axis) were plotted for all the genes (nonessential [negative] controls, common essential [positive] controls and test genes) that were targeted by the minipool library for all the five models screened in minipool format under the same conditions as the genome-wide screens. This showed high concordance (r > 0.59) between the genome-wide screen result and minipool screen result for all these pairs. d,e, Impact of the growth format and culture medium on the Chronos gene effect of representative genes measured by the minipool screen. All genes with significant differential dependency across conditions, in addition to CDK4 and GPX4, are shown. In d, the Chronos gene effects under the Matrigel dome culture (dome) and adherent culture on a plastic plate (plastic) were plotted for pairs of matched screens (same model type, same culture medium; n = 7). Results from cultures with the RPMI/FBS medium and OPAC medium are indicated by the blue and orange points, respectively. In e, the Chronos gene effects under cultures with the RPMI/FBS medium (serum-containing) and OPAC medium (serum-free) were plotted for pairs of matched screens (same model type, same growth format; n = 5). Results from the dome culture and plastic culture are indicated by the blue and orange points, respectively. In both plots, the results from the same pair are indicated by the connecting line and the red horizontal line indicates the median gene effect score of the sample group. The statistical significance of the difference in the gene effects under dome vs plastic cultures as well as under RPMI/FBS vs OPAC media, assessed by a two-tailed paired t-test, are also indicated. Genes whose dependency was significantly (P < 0.05) affected by the growth formats (dome vs plastic) or culture media (RPMI/FBS vs OPAC) are indicated by the asterisks. The dependencies on three integrin subunits (ITGB1, ITGAV and ITGA3) and NCKAP1, a regulator of cytoskeletal organization, were specifically influenced by the growth format but not by the culture medium. In contrast, the dependencies on three mediators of lipid metabolism (SPTLC1, SPTLC2 and SQLE) and RICTOR, an oncogene in the PI3K/mTOR pathway, were affected by the culture medium but not by the growth format. The dependency on PTK2, a gene encoding FAK—a central mediator of adhesion-dependent signaling, was significantly altered by both growth format and culture medium. Here and in f, g, the centre line and box denote median and 25th to 75th percentile data points, respectively, and whiskers extend up to 1.5 times the interquartile range from box edges. f,g, The effects of growth formats and culture media on gene dependencies per gene class. Differential dependencies resulting from different growth formats (dome - plastic; f) and different culture media (OPAC - RPMI/FBS; g), both calculated as coordinates in a linear regression model (see Methods), were plotted for each of the nine classes (see a) of minipool test genes. None of these gene classes exhibited significant enrichment or depletion of dependencies (relative to the rest of the test genes) upon changes in growth format or culture medium, except for the effect of culture medium on ‘Actin Regulation’ gene dependencies, which were enhanced with the RPMI/FBS medium as compared to the OPAC medium (q = 0.0224 by a two-tailed Mann-Whitney U test; indicated by the asterisk). n = 75 (actin regulation), 30 (adherens junction), 53 (cell cycle), 89 (ECM adhesion), 22 (integrin), 20 (lipid metabolism), 20 (PI3K/mTOR signaling), 34 (tight junction) and 29 (WNT signaling).

PAPER PMC13581617
Derivation at a single facility enabled assessment of parameters associated with success (Extended Data Fig. 1 and Supplementary Table 1). The most common reasons for model failure were the development of non-renewable short-term cultures and insufficient tumour cellularity (labelled ‘lack of cells’; Extended Data Fig. 1a). When we account for these factors, by including short-term cultures and excluding samples with low cellularity, our success rate was 65%, comparable to previous studies. Success rates were similar between fresh and frozen tissue (Extended Data Fig. 1b). Once banked, 93% of cultures passed a freeze–thaw quality control step (Fig. 1b), supporting the effectiveness of our protocols to generate renewable cultures. Early passaging and cryopreservation (days to passage: P = 0.0167; days to bank: P = 0.0072; passages to bank: P = 0.0228; Wilcoxon rank-sum test) were unexpectedly associated with banking failure, potentially owing to the dominant outgrowth of non-tumour cells (Extended Data Fig. 1c–e).
These comprehensive genomic and transcriptomic analyses underscore the utility of organoids in reflecting inter-tumour heterogeneity, lineage-specific driver mutations, mutational patterns and transcriptional programs associated with disease and that can be linked to clinical parameters. This study is one of the most comprehensive genomic analyses of organoids performed to date, both in terms of number of models and depth of characterization. To facilitate accessibility of these data, we have made them available through the Cell Model Passports database40 (https://cellmodelpassports.sanger.ac.uk/).
a. Overlap of single guide RNAs (sgRNAs) in the Human CRISPR-Cas9 Libraries Yusa v.1.1 (v1.1) and the Minimal Genome-Wide (MinLibCas9). Only genes with at least two shared sgRNAs were included in the analysis. b. Pearson correlation between two independent batches of Cas9-expressing organoids. Normalised gene log fold changes (LFCs) values are shown for screens performed in three independent organoid cultures. c. Quality control (QC) threshold based on distribution of distances between sgRNA fold changes of replicates from the same organoid (dark grey) and random pairs (light grey), considering the best correlated 800 sgRNAs. d. Pearson correlation between screens performed in three organoids using gene LFCs normalised to the sgRNA library plasmid (x-axis) versus a five days post transduction (early timepoint) control (y-axis). Genes are grouped by whether they target a known essential, non-essential or other gene. e. Comparison of normalised read counts in the plasmid or early time point control, or at the end of screening (Rep1 shown; total technical replicates, n = 3). Data are representative of three organoids. f. The distribution of CRISPRcleanR normalised and corrected LFCs in essential, non-essential, and other genes. Data from all organoids (n = 162) are shown. Light grey dashed lines indicate −1 and 0 LFCs. g and h. Pearson correlation between the number of genes considered depleted by BAGEL2 (x-axis), and the standard deviation (SD) of LFCs or the distance between replicates (y-axis). Dot colour indicates the library tested in each model. Pearson correlations are shown where relevant. All box plots display the median, first and third quartiles (boxes), and whiskers are 1.5x the interquartile range from the first and third quartiles.
We compiled a list of 783 cancer driver genes, derived as the union of two complementary gene sets from the IntOGen78 and COSMIC79 databases, as previously reported9. Each gene was annotated by its mechanism of action as either activating (Act, for oncogenes), loss-of-function (LoF, for tumour suppressor genes), ambiguous (with evidence of both mechanisms), or fusion. The complete list of cancer driver genes is in https://cellmodelpassports.sanger.ac.uk/downloads.
Paired-end transcriptome reads (75 bp) were quality filtered and mapped to GRCh38 (ensemble build 98) using STAR (v2.5.0c)90 with a standard set of parameters (https://github.com/cancerit/cgpRna). Resulting bam files were processed to get the per gene read count and transcripts per million (TPM) data using RSEM (v1.3.3)91. TPM values were used for the downstream analysis.
Consensus molecular subtypes (CMS) and colorectal cancer intrinsic subtypes (CRIS) subtypes were inferred for COAD/READ organoids with CMSCaller36,92 using RSEM expected count data.
CRISPR screens performed using the Yusa v1.1 (ref. 3) and MinLibCas9 (ref. 41) libraries were harmonized by restricting analyses to shared sgRNAs, enabling integrated downstream processing. Quality control procedures, adapted from established cell line screening pipelines3, were applied to assess replicate concordance, sgRNA representation and classifier performance in distinguishing essential from non-essential genes. Low-quality replicates and organoids failing predefined quality control criteria were excluded prior to further analysis. Read counts were normalized and corrected for copy-number effects using CRISPRcleanR (v3.0.1)96, followed by batch correction across libraries97. Gene-level fitness effects were estimated using BAGEL (v2)45 with curated reference gene sets, and LFC values were scaled relative to essential and non-essential controls to facilitate cross-organoid comparability. Full details of sgRNA selection, quality control thresholds, normalization, batch correction, statistical procedures and final selection of models are provided in the Supplementary Information.
For high-throughput screening, organoids were dissociated into single cells, counted and seeded in 5% BME-2 suspension cultures at model-specific optimized densities. After 96 h to allow organoid re-formation, assay plates were prepared with a 50% BME-2:organoid medium base layer, and organoids were transferred into 384-well plates using Multidrop Combi (Thermo Scientific) dispensers. Twenty-four hours later, compounds were dispensed using an Echo555 (Labcyte), and cells were treated for 72 h. Viability was measured using CellTiter-Glo 2.0 (Promega).
The raw WGS, targeted gene sequencing (TGS) and RNA-seq data from organoids used in this study, along with matched tumour tissues and normal samples, have been deposited in the European genome-phenome archive (EGA): WGS, EGAD00001015469; TGS, EGAD0000101546; RNA-seq, EGAD00001015470. Main results of the analysis are presented in the Supplementary Tables. In addition, genomic, transcriptomic and CRISPR processed data are available in the Cell Model Passports40 and DepMap miner database. Additional datasets required to reproduce figures and analyses presented in this Article are available on Figshare (https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.28339340 (ref. 103)). Public datasets used in this study include resources from IntOGen78, the COSMIC Cancer Gene Census79, the Cancer Genome Interpreter and BoostDM78, MSKCC Cancer Hotspots80, the Cancer Predisposition Variant dataset81, the ENCODE blacklist84 and COSMIC mutational signatures v3.430. Additional curated data from large-scale CRISPR screens and cancer genomics studies were obtained from Pacini et al.9. An overview of all tables and datasets used in this study, including their location, source and associated analyses, is provided in Supplementary Table 10.

PAPER PMC13664187
Identifying genes cancer cells need to survive or grow may help researchers choose experimental targets. Direct testing is difficult and costly in organoids, which are small three-dimensional models grown from patient tumors. Using gene activity measurements from 852 conventional cancer models, we built predictive models for a preselected panel of 20 genes. Our primary evaluation asked whether the models could rank organoids by their dependence on each gene. Performance was weaker in organoids, but a small signal remained in 94 Broad organoids and in 148 Sanger organoids from 144 donors. Predictions for the Sanger organoids were fixed before the measured outcomes were accessed. A separate retrospective analysis examined whether the same predictions could rank the 20 genes within each organoid. This retrospective analysis showed modest improvement over tumor-lineage predictions for the full ranking, while improvement for the three strongest measured dependencies was observed only in Sanger. Pancreatic organoid results remained projections because direct measurements were unavailable. These findings provide limited evidence for ranking genes within the selected panel. They do not establish which experiments will succeed or which treatments will work in patients.
Transferability of transcriptome-based gene-dependency models from two-dimensional cancer models to organoids remains uncertain. Expression-only Elastic Net models for 20 preselected genes were selected using patient-grouped nested cross-validation in 681 models and evaluated in a locked 171-model holdout. Historical target selection used all 852 models, making holdout performance conditional internal confirmation rather than an unbiased generalization estimate. After refitting on all 852 models, frozen models underwent exploratory evaluation in 94 Broad organoids and blinded validation in 148 Sanger organoids from 144 donors. Predictions were sealed before Sanger outcome access. Primary target-wise Spearman correlations, ranking organoids per gene, averaged 0.4094, 0.1518, and 0.1015 in holdout, Broad, and Sanger, respectively. Sanger association exceeded the within-lineage permutation null (p = 0.00040), but improvement over lineage-only prediction remained uncertain (difference, 0.0462; 95% confidence interval, −0.0065–0.0986). CDK6, MDM4, and CCNE1 passed false discovery rate correction in Sanger. Retrospective within-organoid gene ranking modestly improved over lineage-only prediction in both cohorts; top-three overlap improved only in Sanger. Pancreatic results remained unvalidated projections. Findings support weak cross-domain rank transfer and limited within-organoid ranking within the selected panel, without establishing genome-wide transferability, causal explanations for attenuation, or prospective experimental or therapeutic utility.
Despite these advances, the extent to which transcriptome-dependency relationships learned in 2D cancer cell lines transfer to independent 3D organoid cohorts remains unresolved. A rigorous assessment requires model selection to be completed without access to 3D outcomes, predictions to be locked before outcome evaluation, and performance to be tested across independently generated CRISPR datasets. Here, we developed target-specific, expression-based Elastic Net models for a fixed panel of 20 genes using 852 pan-cancer 2D models. Candidate molecular representations were compared using patient-grouped nested cross-validation. The selected model was evaluated once in a locked 2D holdout for internal confirmation conditional on historical target selection. Without outcome-guided retraining, the frozen model was then examined exploratorily in Broad organoids and subjected to blinded independent external validation in a non-overlapping Sanger organoid cohort. Target-level findings from the two 3D cohorts were integrated in a retrospective transportability synthesis, and the locked models were subsequently projected onto an independent pancreatic organoid RNA-seq cohort lacking measured CRISPR outcomes. The primary hypothesis concerned ranking organoids by measured dependency for each gene, not ranking genes within an individual organoid. We hypothesized that this between-organoid signal would remain detectable but attenuated in 3D systems. A separate retrospective analysis evaluated gene ranking within individual organoids using the same frozen predictions. This secondary analysis addressed a different prediction task and did not replace the original primary evaluation.
A separate Elastic Net regression model was fitted for each of the 20 targets and each molecular representation [21]. The frozen search space was l1_ratio = {0.10, 0.50, 0.90} and alpha = {0.001, 0.01, 0.1}; we used a maximum of 20,000 iterations, a convergence tolerance of 0.0001, and cyclic coordinate updates. The inner objective was the mean target-specific Spearman correlation across the five inner-validation folds. We prioritized settings that converged in all inner folds; among them, we selected the setting with the highest mean inner Spearman correlation, resolving remaining ties in favor of a larger alpha and then a lower l1_ratio. When no setting converged in all five folds, we selected based on the number of converged folds, mean inner Spearman correlation, and larger alpha. We refit the chosen setting on the complete outer-training partition and applied it once to the outer-test partition. For the lineage-only reference, target-specific ridge regression was fitted to the 29 lineage indicators, with alpha = {0.1, 1, 10, 100} compared in the inner validation.
Predictions from the five outer-test folds were combined to generate out-of-fold (OOF) predictions for all 681 development models. The primary candidate score was the arithmetic mean of the 20 target-specific Spearman correlations, termed the macro target-wise Spearman correlation. We retained target-specific mean absolute error (MAE), root mean squared error (RMSE), coefficient of determination (R2), calibration intercept, and calibration slope as secondary diagnostics, which did not alter model selection. Stability of the target-specific feature sets across outer folds was summarized using pairwise Jaccard similarity.
Candidate selection used only the development OOF predictions. Uncertainty for each candidate was estimated from 2000 bootstrap resamples of PatientID clusters. The one-standard-error threshold was defined as the highest observed molecular-candidate OOF macro Spearman correlation minus the candidate’s bootstrap standard error. Among molecular candidates meeting this threshold, we selected the least complex representation according to the prespecified order: expression-only, expression + copy number, expression + mutation, and all omics + lineage. Lineage-only was a non-molecular reference and was excluded from this rule.
Table S3 further documents the development lock. For the selected candidate, the final l1_ratio and alpha pair for each target was the setting chosen most often across outer folds; ties were resolved by higher mean inner Spearman correlation, then by larger alpha. The 300 target-specific features were relearned using all 681 development models. Before indexing the 171-model outcome matrix for performance evaluation, we sealed the frozen protocol, split registry, selected representation, target-specific feature identities, hyperparameters, model manifest, and locked-holdout predictions using separate SHA-256 digests. No candidate, target, feature, hyperparameter, or primary metric was changed thereafter, and no 3D data were accessed during development.
The locked holdout outcomes were evaluated once after the prediction lock. The primary endpoint was the macro target-wise Spearman correlation. We calculated target-specific Spearman correlation, MAE, RMSE, R2, calibration intercept, and calibration slope as secondary diagnostics. We estimated uncertainty in the macro Spearman correlation from 2000 bootstrap resamples of PatientID clusters; we retained all rows belonging to a sampled patient group together, and the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles defined the 95% confidence interval (CI). The holdout-minus-development difference distribution was obtained by subtracting the development OOF bootstrap value from the holdout bootstrap value within each iteration. No model, feature, hyperparameter, or metric was changed during evaluation. Because historical target-panel selection used all 852 models, this stage provided internal confirmation conditional on that selection. We did not interpret its performance as an unbiased generalization estimate. The patient-cluster bootstrap did not account for uncertainty or optimism arising from historical target selection.
To evaluate information beyond tumor lineage, the lineage-only ridge reference described in Section 2.4 was refitted in the 681 development models and applied to the same 171 holdout models. The difference in macro Spearman correlation between the selected model and the lineage-only reference was estimated using common PatientID-cluster bootstrap resamples. When more than one model was associated with a patient, observed outcomes and predictions were additionally averaged at the PatientID level. With predictions fixed, complete 20-target outcome vectors were permuted among patient groups within each OncotreeLineage stratum for 2000 iterations. The one-sided empirical p-value was calculated as (1 + the number of null statistics greater than or equal to the observed statistic)/2001. The patient-aggregated macro Spearman correlation was retained as a sensitivity estimate.
The primary estimand was the macro target-wise Spearman correlation. We calculated target-specific Spearman correlation, MAE, RMSE, R2, calibration intercept, and calibration slope as secondary diagnostics and did not use them to reopen model selection. We estimated macro- and target-specific uncertainty from 5000 model-level bootstrap resamples performed with replacement within OncotreeLineage strata, preserving the observed lineage counts; 95% CIs were defined by the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. The lineage-only ridge reference was applied to the same 94 organoids, and the expression-only-minus-lineage difference was estimated using paired bootstrap resamples. Target-specific partial Spearman correlations, adjusted for lineage indicators; lineage subgroup estimates for groups containing at least 7 models; and leave-one-lineage-out analyses were retained as secondary sensitivity analyses.
For the panel-wide permutation test, predictions were held fixed, and each organoid’s complete 20-target outcome vector was permuted among model rows within the same OncotreeLineage stratum for 5000 iterations, thereby preserving cross-target dependence and lineage composition. The one-sided empirical p-value was calculated as (1 + the number of null macro Spearman statistics greater than or equal to the observed statistic)/5001. Target-specific permutation p-values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg false discovery rate (FDR) procedure. All Broad resampling procedures used random seed 20260829. As specified in Table S5, rank transportability was the primary endpoint. Absolute-scale error, calibration, and lineage sensitivity were secondary diagnostics. Broad results were not used to retrain the model or select targets based on effect direction or statistical significance.
The primary external-validation estimand was the target-specific Spearman correlation between the blinded predictions and Sanger LFC values, followed by the arithmetic mean of the 20 target correlations (macro target-wise Spearman correlation). This primary estimand evaluates the ordering of organoids for a fixed gene and does not assess the ordering of genes within an organoid. Rank correlation was selected because the Sanger-corrected LFC and DepMap/Broad Chronos Gene Effect values are on assay-specific scales and were not assumed to be numerically comparable. Uncertainty was estimated with 5000 donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, retaining all organoids from a sampled donor together; 95% CIs were obtained from the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles. For the null distribution, repeated organoids were first averaged within donor, predictions were held fixed, and each donor-level 20-target observed-outcome vector was permuted within tumor-type/lineage strata for 5000 iterations. The two-sided empirical p-value was calculated as (1 + the number of absolute null macro correlations at least as large as the observed absolute macro correlation)/5001. Target-specific permutation p-values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure.
A lineage-only comparator, frozen before access to Sanger outcomes and based on patient-weighted 2D lineage means, was applied to the same cohort. We evaluated the expression-only-minus-lineage macro difference using paired donor-cluster bootstrap resamples, and we also calculated partial Spearman correlations adjusted for the primary tumor-type indicators. The influence of repeated donors was examined in two additional analyses: one retained the lexicographically first sample identifier per donor, and the other averaged predictions and outcomes within donor. An independent replay recomputed cohort size, donor count, overlap exclusions, feature coverage, target and prediction order, the complete 2960-pair grid, correlation statistics, bootstrap and permutation results, lineage adjustment, and file hashes; all 22 checks passed. As detailed in Table S6, no model, target, feature, hyperparameter, or prespecified primary metric was changed after outcome unlock. The retrospective within-organoid endpoints were evaluated separately using the unchanged predictions.
Directional concordance was defined as a positive Spearman correlation for the same target in both Broad-94 and Sanger-148. The number of common-positive targets was compared with a one-sided exact binomial distribution with n = 20 and a null success probability of 0.50. This comparison was interpreted only as a descriptive sign benchmark: dependence among targets limits the binomial interpretation, and the synthesis was defined after the cohort-specific results were known. Agreement in relative target performance was evaluated separately using the two-sided Spearman correlation between the vectors of 20 Broad and 20 Sanger target-specific correlations.
For target-level pooling, each cohort-specific Spearman correlation was transformed to the Fisher-z scale. We derived inverse-variance weights from the cohort-specific uncertainty intervals obtained from the lineage-stratified Broad bootstrap and the donor-clustered Sanger bootstrap. Because Broad donor structure was incompletely ascertained, its original uncertainty weights may overstate precision; the synthesis is conditional on this unresolved model-level independence assumption. A fixed-effects pooled estimate was calculated for each target and then transformed back to the Spearman correlation scale. The resulting two-sided meta-analytic p-values were adjusted across the 20 targets using the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR procedure. Because only two 3D cohorts were available, random-effects estimates and the I2 heterogeneity statistic were retained as descriptive sensitivity measures and were not used as confirmatory decision criteria.
The 20-target median projection vector for Tiriac tumor cases was compared separately with the corresponding median prediction vectors for Broad-94 and Sanger-148. This comparison assessed whether the projected pancreatic target hierarchy was consistent across organoid applications. Concordance was quantified using two-sided Spearman correlations across the 20 targets. Because all compared values were predictions from the same locked model family and Tiriac lacked measured CRISPR outcomes, these correlations were interpreted as secondary evidence of target-ranking consistency rather than independent evidence of predictive accuracy.
Statistical analyses preserved the prespecified role and strongest available biological independence unit of each study layer. In the development, locked 2D holdout, Broad-94, and Sanger-148 analyses, the primary rank-based measure was the Spearman correlation between predicted and observed outcomes for each target and the arithmetic mean of the 20 target correlations (macro target-wise Spearman correlation). MAE, RMSE, R2, calibration intercept, and calibration slope were secondary diagnostics in the development, locked-holdout, and Broad analyses and did not reopen model selection. Because Sanger-corrected LFC and DepMap/Broad Chronos Gene Effect values were not treated as numerically equivalent, the Sanger validation and cross-cohort synthesis were based on rank correlation rather than absolute-scale error. Target-wise correlation operates across organoids for each gene; within-organoid correlation instead operates across the 20 genes for each organoid. Averaging target-wise correlations does not test within-organoid gene ranking. The original analysis hierarchy, inferential units, resampling procedures, and multiplicity rules are summarized in Table S9; the retrospective within-organoid endpoints are defined in Section 2.10.1 and Tables S17–S19.
In the Tiriac layer, two-sided Mann–Whitney U tests compared patient-level tumor and normal projections for each target. Benjamini–Hochberg FDR adjustment was applied across 20 tests. Cliff’s delta described effect direction. Concordance between the Tiriac tumor target-median vector and the Broad and Sanger target-median prediction vectors was evaluated using two-sided Spearman correlations across 20 targets. We used PCA and SMD measures only as descriptive domain-shift diagnostics, and they did not inform the confirmatory decision.
A retrospective secondary analysis evaluated whether the frozen expression-only predictions ranked the 20 genes within individual organoids in agreement with measured CRISPR outcomes. This secondary analysis was defined after the external outcomes were available and was not a new blinded validation. It retained all 20 targets and all 94 Broad and 148 Sanger organoids; Tiriac was excluded because measured dependency outcomes were unavailable. No predictions, models, features, hyperparameters, or targets were changed, and the original target-wise primary analysis was retained.
For each organoid, we calculated Spearman correlation across its 20 predicted and 20 observed scores, using average ranks for ties, and summarized the correlations by their arithmetic mean. Lower scores denoted stronger measured or predicted dependency. The main comparator assigned every organoid the same per-gene mean of the observed outcomes across all 852 2D training models, with each training model weighted equally. This fixed ranking tested whether expression predictions improved on gene-level average dependency. We also reused the archived lineage-only predictions: the ridge reference in Broad and patient-weighted 2D lineage means in Sanger. No external outcomes were used to fit either comparator (Table S17).
Using the same frozen models, we compared training-median imputation with two alternatives: zero on the log2(TPM + 1) scale before standardization, and the stored training-reference mean for each feature. These means were estimated after median imputation in the 852-model training data and correspond to zero standardized input, up to floating-point rounding. Only absent features were replaced; observed expression values, including measured zeros, were retained. We summarized absolute projection changes, Spearman correlation across the 20 genes within each profile, and top-three set overlap relative to the median-imputed reference. We evaluated sample-level results and case-averaged profiles, including 38 tumor and 11 normal cases. We also repeated the original tumor–normal comparisons using the same 5000 bootstrap resamples (seed 20260830), Mann–Whitney tests, and 20-target FDR adjustment. These are descriptive checks of projection stability, not prediction accuracy. Because the models are linear and the missing-feature pattern is shared, constant replacement values create a common offset per target; preservation of within-target sample ranks and tumor–normal differences is therefore expected algebraically (Tables S20 and S21).
Five input representations—the lineage-only reference and four molecular candidates—were compared in the development partition of 681 models representing 658 patient groups, using identical patient-grouped outer folds. Candidate-level OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlations and paired differences relative to expression-only are shown in Figure 2a,b; full candidate- and target-level results are provided in Table S10.

(b) Paired differences in macro Spearman correlation between each augmented molecular representation and expression-only. As shown in Figure 2a, the highest observed OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlation was obtained with expression + copy number (ρ = 0.4081; 95% CI, 0.3894–0.4243). Performance was nearly identical for expression-only (ρ = 0.4071; 95% CI, 0.3893–0.4232), expression + mutation (ρ = 0.4073; 95% CI, 0.3908–0.4235), and all omics + lineage (ρ = 0.4031; 95% CI, 0.3864–0.4184). The lineage-only reference produced a lower macro correlation (ρ = 0.2598; 95% CI, 0.2388–0.2806) and, as prespecified, was not eligible for molecular candidate selection.

(c) Target-specific locked-holdout correlations, ordered from lowest to highest; all 20 were positive. (d) Finalization sequence. After the one-time holdout evaluation, the winning model was refitted on all 852 two-dimensional models while keeping the target panel, target-specific feature identities, hyperparameters, and primary metric fixed; all 20 deployment models converged before three-dimensional use. The target panel had been selected using the full source population, so holdout performance represents internal confirmation conditional on that selection, not an unbiased generalization estimate. As shown in Figure 3a,b, the development OOF macro target-wise Spearman correlation was 0.4071 (95% CI, 0.3893–0.4232), and the locked-holdout value was 0.4094 (95% CI, 0.3730–0.4383). The holdout-minus-development difference was +0.0023 (95% CI, −0.0372 to 0.0361). The interval included zero, indicating no detectable difference within this preselected panel. This comparison does not remove the dependence introduced by historical target selection.

Error bars show 95% bootstrap confidence intervals where available; the lineage-adjusted estimate is shown without an interval. Broad-94 remained exploratory and was not treated as independent external validation. As shown in Figure 4a,c,d, the macro target-wise Spearman correlation in Broad-94 was 0.1518 (95% lineage-stratified bootstrap CI, 0.1019–0.2006). Eighteen of 20 target correlations were positive, and the median target correlation was 0.1628. The Broad-94 macro correlation was 0.2576 lower than the locked 2D holdout value. In the within-lineage permutation analysis, an association at least as large as the observed value occurred rarely (one-sided p = 0.00080), supporting partial but attenuated transfer of rank information.
The lineage-adjusted macro Spearman correlation was 0.1080, whereas the lineage-only reference yielded 0.0951. The paired expression-only-minus-lineage-only difference was +0.0567 (95% CI, 0.0040–0.1106). Thus, lineage composition did not fully explain the exploratory association, although it accounted for part of the observed signal.
At the target level, the highest correlations were observed for ERBB3 (ρ = 0.3489), CCNE1 (ρ = 0.3456), CTNNB1 (ρ = 0.3009), E2F3 (ρ = 0.2679), and YAP1 (ρ = 0.2614) (Figure 4b; Table S12). MAPK1 (ρ = −0.0307) and STK11 (ρ = −0.1010) were the only targets with negative correlations. After adjustment of the 20 target-level permutation p values, no target met the Benjamini–Hochberg FDR threshold of q < 0.05; the lowest adjusted value was q = 0.0640 for CCNE1. Accordingly, Broad-94 was interpreted at the fixed-panel level rather than as confirmatory evidence for individual targets.
The donor audit identified one confirmed same-donor pair among 25 models with donor information; 69 models remained unresolved (Table S22). Omitting ACH-002883 or ACH-002884 gave macro target-wise correlations of 0.1516 and 0.1539, respectively, compared with the original 0.1518. Under the conditional full-cohort bootstrap, the expression-only estimate was 0.1518 (0.1009 to 0.2007) and its paired difference from lineage-only prediction was 0.0567 (0.0027 to 0.1081) (estimate and 95% CI; Table S23). These findings quantify sensitivity to the identified pair, conditional on treating the unresolved models as separate units.
In the 25-model donor-documented subset, the expression-only macro correlation was 0.0741 (−0.0154 to 0.1618), with an expression-minus-lineage difference of −0.0571 (−0.1502 to 0.0305) under source-specific donor-cluster resampling. Both intervals included zero, so this subset did not establish superiority over lineage. Averaging the vectors within the 24 source-specific donor groups gave 0.0727 (−0.0195 to 0.1629) for expression-only prediction. This subset is not a representative replacement for Broad-94; changes relative to the full cohort can reflect sample and lineage composition as well as grouping. Paired within-subset model-versus-cluster intervals and secondary ranking sensitivities are provided in Tables S23 and S24. Incomplete donor ascertainment prevents these analyses from verifying full-cohort independence or providing a fully donor-adjusted effective sample size.
